Abstract

This report documents, end to end, a three-node research programme that asks a narrow question: do short-horizon geometric patterns in the Deriv Jump 10 index repeat often enough, and with enough conditional structure, to support a positive-expectation options strategy after realistic frictions? It answers that question in the negative, and — more usefully — documents exactly where the illusion of an edge came from.

The corpus is 591,171 consecutive 150-tick cells carved from 88,675,650 midpoint ticks (2021-03-08 to 2023-12-31). Phase 1 encodes every cell as a 75-harmonic Fourier epicycle series with exact reconstruction (maximum absolute tick error 0.0). Phase 2 decomposes each cell into four structural quartiles, measures relational similarity between the first half of every cell and the first half of every other cell, and selects 11,266 "anomaly hubs" that have unusually many partners. Those hubs and their 241,703 undirected covariant edges form a graph whose second-half behaviour can be measured and — apparently — traded.

Phase 2 produced a strategy vault of 893 anchors reporting out-of-sample win rates from 55% to 1.000 on its thinnest rows, with a 99th percentile of 0.818. Phase 3 re-tested every anchor with a strictly causal, tick-exact replay, a four-contract expected-value auction, four friction tiers, a 5,000-run block bootstrap Monte Carlo, and quarter-Kelly position sizing. The portfolio came out at 0.452 win rate, profit factor 0.958, cumulative equity -86.7σ gross and -209.0σ net of 0.03σ friction, with a Monte-Carlo probability of a positive result of 18.6%.

A dedicated diagnostic then reconciled the contradiction. Phase 2's headline "verified edge" was defined as max(protected_held_win_rate, directional_win_rate). The maximum of two weakly correlated ~50% statistics is upward-biased by construction, and selecting the top of that distribution guarantees an apparent 55–93% edge even when both components are fair coins. Recomputed from scratch on outbound data, the directional win rate is 48.8% and the Phase 2 column itself has a median of 0.500. Phase 3B was nevertheless completed: a seven-guard Deriv WebSocket forward tester with a latency/payout probe, SQLite calibration journal, and a documented live-parity recall of 24.0%. Deriv's public WebSocket returned HTTP 520 from every node during this work, so live payouts remain unverified and the tester is parked in paper-proposal mode.

The report is deliberately negative. It is also, we argue, more valuable than a positive result obtained by loosening a gate: it identifies a reusable failure mode (extremum-of-two-statistics selection) that will silently manufacture "edges" in any vault built this way.

1. Executive summary

Three phases, one question. This chapter states the result in the form a risk committee would want it: what was claimed, what survived, and what it would take to reopen the question.

1.1 The claim under test

Phase 2 concluded that a vault of 893 "covariant anomaly" anchors in Jump 10 showed out-of-sample directional or protected-extremum win rates between 55% and 93%, with 0.687 mean win rate for the top 300 and a selection-aware block-permutation p-value of 0.0025. If true at the top of that range, a binary option paying 1.95× would produce an enormous edge: at 60% win the expected return per unit stake is 0.60×1.953 − 1 = +17.2%.

Phase 3 was built to falsify that claim under conditions as close to live as the data allows: strictly causal features, tick-exact replay of ticks 75–149, a payout-aware contract auction, four friction tiers, non-overlapping trade selection, and a Monte Carlo over trade order.

1.2 What survived

LayerVerdictEvidence
Data integrity (Phase 1)PASS 591,171 cells, 88,675,650 ticks, max tick reconstruction error 0.0, max norm RMSE 2.353e-15, contiguous across all three node boundaries.
Graph structure (Phase 2)REAL 11,266 hubs, 241,703 undirected edges, median peer count 60, median mean |r| 0.862; independently re-audited on two other machines.
Conditional ranking (CZET v2)REAL Spearman 0.5111 vs cov baseline 0.1006; R² 0.02825. Ranking skill, not magnitude.
Directional trade edgeFAILS Outbound directional win 45.2% net of friction; breakeven at 1.953× is 51.2%. Recomputed from scratch on the same features: 48.8%.
Protected-extremum trade edgeFAILS NOTOUCH realised 30–33% where 64.5% is required at 1.55×.
Phase 2 "verified edge"ARTIFACT Defined as max(protected, directional); per-anchor correlation between the stored column and a faithful recomputation is -0.049; the stored column's own median is 0.500.
Live readiness (Phase 3B)BLOCKED Tester complete and self-checked; Deriv WebSocket returns HTTP 520 from all three cluster nodes, so live payouts could not be verified.

1.4 Why the result is negative, in one paragraph

Cosine similarity between 132-dimensional whitened cell descriptors is dominated by a shared common-mode direction: 37% of arbitrary outbound cells sit within 0.85–0.955 of at least one of 893 anchors, so "matching an anchor" is closer to "being a Jump-10 cell" than to "repeating a specific anomaly". Once the matching is made causal and de-duplicated, the conditional distribution of the next 75 ticks is indistinguishable from the unconditional one in the direction that the vault trades. The Phase 2 statistics that looked decisive were computed as the maximum of two ~50% quantities, which is upward-biased by selection and by small-sample variance.

1.5 What this report contains

Chapters 2–3 describe the market mechanics and the cluster. Chapters 4–11 document Phase 1 and Phase 2 with the actual artifacts. Chapters 12–17 document Phase 3, including the diagnostic that reconciled the contradiction and the live tester. Chapters 18–20 cover reproducibility, limitations and the roadmap. The appendices reproduce the source listings, data dictionaries, metric formulas and a checksum manifest, so the report can be audited without the original machines.

2. Market mechanics and edge mathematics

A backtest is only meaningful relative to a contract. This chapter fixes the instrument, the tick sampler, the contract families, and the arithmetic that every later gate depends on.

2.1 The instrument

Jump 10 Index is a synthetic index published by Deriv that follows a geometric random walk with a constant volatility plus periodic discrete jumps. It ticks 24/7 and each tick carries a timestamp and a bid/ask. We use the midpoint mid = (bid+ask)/2 as the price series; this is recorded in the catalog column price_source for every cell, and no cell mixes sources.

Jump 10 is attractive for this research because it is stationary in its volatility parameter: there is no session structure, no overnight gap, and no earnings calendar. Any apparent edge must therefore come from path geometry rather than from time-of-day or news effects. That property is what makes a negative result credible — there is no convenient confound to blame.

2.2 Cell definition

The unit of analysis is a cell: 150 consecutive ticks, numbered 1..150. Cells are cut contiguously from the tick stream with no overlap and no gaps; the catalog records tick_start_idx and tick_end_idx so any cell can be reconstructed exactly. Each cell is then split into four quartiles of 37–38 ticks (Q1..Q4), and into two halves of 75 ticks. The two halves have a specific operational meaning:

  • Ticks 0–74 (the "0–50% window") is the only observable part at decision time.
  • Ticks 75–149 (the "50–100% window") is the forward path that a trade must be settled against. Every Phase 3 outcome is a function of this window only.

Cells at the very end of the corpus whose 150th tick does not exist are dropped, not padded; the corpus therefore ends at 2023-12-31.

2.3 Contract families

Deriv offers several contract structures on synthetic indices. Phase 3 evaluates four, because they monetise different properties of the forward path:

ContractWins when Assumed payoutBreakeven win rate What it monetises
RISE_FALLclose149 > 0 after polarity alignment 1.953×51.20%Directional drift over the second half.
ONETOUCH_075max excursion ≥ +0.75σ2.150× 46.51%Whether the path reaches a barrier at all.
BARRIER_035close149 ≥ +0.35σ 2.427×41.20%Direction plus minimum displacement.
NOTOUCH_10min excursion > −1.0σ1.550× 64.52%The "protected extremum" claim: the path never breaks the far barrier.

The payout column is an assumption inherited from the Phase 3 brief, and Section 14.4 shows that at least one entry is arithmetically inconsistent with the barrier it is paired with. It is the single most load-bearing assumption in the report, and Chapter 16 explains why it could not be verified against Deriv's live pricing. Every conclusion that does not depend on it is stated in σ units instead of dollars.

2.4 The arithmetic every gate reduces to

For a contract paying R per unit stake and winning with probability p, the expected value per unit stake is

EV = p·R − 1

and the breakeven probability is p* = 1/R. A gate that requires EV > +0.02 therefore requires

p > 1.02 / R

Phase 3 applies this to a lower confidence bound on p, not to the point estimate: the Wilson 95% lower bound of the anchor's inbound hit rate. This is deliberately conservative — an anchor with 30 inbound hits and a 60% point estimate has a Wilson bound near 0.43, which clears the 2.427× barrier gate but not the 1.953× gate.

2.5 Frictions

Four friction tiers are applied in σ units, where σ is the cell's own causal half-window standard deviation: 0.00 (gross), 0.03 (realistic Jump-10 spread relative to a 75-tick move), 0.06 and 0.10 (stress). Friction is charged on the realised excursion, not on the stake, which is the correct model for a binary where the spread is embedded in the entry price. The friction ladder must be monotone; the gatekeeper asserts it.

2.6 Why σ normalisation matters

Jump 10's absolute tick size drifts with the index level. A 0.35-point barrier means something completely different at 8,000 and at 101,000. Normalising every path by the first-half standard deviation makes barrier distances dimensionless and comparable across two and a half years of data. It also makes the barrier levels in the contract table above interpretable: +0.75σ is "three quarters of the typical half-window move".

3. Cluster architecture

The work is compute-bound in a way that punishes serial execution: 591,171 cells times 893 anchors is 528 million pairwise similarities per pass, and Phase 3 replays every tick. The cluster exists to make that cheap without freezing the master workstation.

3.1 Nodes

RoleHostOS RAMContribution
Master / orchestratorX-Flip 14 192.168.150.199 Windows 11—Feature build, merge, reporting, MC, documentation, MT5 bridge.
Worker AHP 840 G7 QUISON 192.168.150.126 Windows 1115.7 GBPhase 1 shard 2 (209,757 cells), Phase 2 DB audit, Phase 3 shard 1.
Worker BKali Dell E5270 192.168.150.166 Kali Linux7.7 GBPhase 1 shard 3 (209,793 cells), edge-graph audit, Phase 3 shard 2.

3.2 Control plane

Tasks are dispatched over TCP 5555 by cluster_ctl.py, which opens a command channel, streams stdout, and enforces a 30-second timeout for fast_cmd jobs. Because the worker-side agent is an LLM that sometimes rewrites complex quoting, the reliable pattern is to write a .py helper on the worker and invoke it by path rather than to nest quotes. Long jobs are detached (start /b ... > log 2>&1 on Windows, nohup ... & on Linux) and polled through their log file.

3.3 Data plane

Bulk transfer uses a single HTTP server on port 8080 (phase1_master_server.py) that serves /out/* and accepts PUT /upload/*, creating parent directories as needed. Directory junctions (phase1/out/phase2 → C:\cluster\phase2) let workers address phase directories by a stable relative path. This avoided installing anything on the workers and kept the attack surface to one port.

3.4 Reproducibility constraints

  • Deterministic sharding. Every phase divides cells by absolute index, not by file, so the node boundaries are identical on every run. Phase 1 boundaries are cells 171,621 and 381,378; Phase 3 uses a round-robin over the anchor list.
  • Identical σ binning on all nodes. Phase 3 computes sigma quantiles from the causal inbound set and prints them; all three nodes produced [0.1526, 0.1755, 0.1772, 0.1787, 0.1805, 0.1912] bps, which is the strongest available proof that the shards saw the same data.
  • Honest heterogeneity. Kali has no duckdb, so it emits CSV where Windows emits parquet. The merger reads both. This is a feature: it proves the merge is not format-coupled.

3.5 Failure modes encountered (and their fixes)

SymptomCauseFix
cencoding.pyd blockedWindows Application Control blocks fastparquet's native module (pyarrow was already blocked). pqcompat.py prefers duckdb, then pyarrow, then fastparquet; all parquet I/O goes through it.
Master unreachable after rebootDHCP moved the master's LAN address three times (.237 → .157 → .199).xfer.py probes a candidate list and auto-detects.
Worker agent rewrites commandsThe remote agent is an LLM and normalises quoting.Ship a script, invoke by path; never nest triple quotes.
Parquet from Kali missingNo duckdb in the Kali venv.CSV fallback plus a merger that globs both extensions.

4. Phase 1 - corpus construction

Phase 1 turns a raw tick export into 591,171 numbered, reconstructible cells. It is the only phase whose output is treated as ground truth by everything downstream.

4.1 Inputs

The raw export is a sequence of CSV files, one per month, containing epoch-millisecond timestamps plus bid and ask. Tick indices are global and 1-based: tick 1 is the first tick of the first file. No rows are skipped, filtered, or de-duplicated — the corpus is the export.

4.2 Sharding

The stream is cut into 150-tick cells up to a global count of 591,171. Shards are assigned by contiguous cell ranges so that each worker can stream its slice of ticks and drop the leading partial cell:

NodeCellsFirst cellLast cellTicks Harmonics KContiguityMax tick errorMax norm RMSE
xflip171,6211171,62125,743,15075True0.02.353e-15
kali209,793381,379591,17131,468,95075True0.02.169e-15
840g7209,757171,622381,37831,463,55075True0.02.338e-15
total591,171 1591,17188,675,650 75all0.0—

4.3 What "exact" means here

Two invariants are asserted per shard and again after the merge:

  • Tick losslessness: the reconstructed price at every tick index in the shard equals the source price bit-for-bit. Measured as max_tick_error, which is 0.0 for all three shards.
  • Norm error: the L2 distance between the 75-harmonic encoding of a cell and the cell itself, divided by the cell's norm. max_norm_rmse is at machine epsilon (2.2–2.4×10-15), and all 591,171 cells are under 1e-11.

4.4 Merge and boundary audit

The merge concatenates cell ranges in order and then verifies the two seams explicitly (node_boundaries in the verification JSON): left cell 171,621 ends on tick 25,743,150 and cell 171,622 begins on 25,743,151; left cell 381,378 ends on 57,206,700 and 381,379 begins on 57,206,701. Both seams report ok: true. A duplicate or missing tick at a seam is therefore impossible.

4.5 The catalog

Every cell also gets a 19-column catalog row: index bounds, open/close timestamps, OHLC, mean and std, the normalised open level, a bridge slope, residual RMSE, the reconstruction errors, and the source file and price source. The catalog is the join key for every later phase — Phase 2 uses it to rebuild time ordering, Phase 3 uses it for the UTC hour audit.

phase1/scripts/phase1_worker.py - shard worker (477 lines, 17.9 KB)
import os
import sys
import json
import time
import math
import gc
import shutil
import subprocess
import traceback
import urllib.request
from pathlib import Path

import numpy as np
import pandas as pd

CELL_SIZE = 150


def log(msg):
    print(f"[{time.strftime('%H:%M:%S')}] {msg}", flush=True)


def json_dump(path, obj):
    tmp = str(path) + ".tmp"
    with open(tmp, "w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        json.dump(obj, f, indent=2)
    os.replace(tmp, path)


def upload_file(local_path, url, retries=5):
    curl = shutil.which("curl") or shutil.which("curl.exe")
    if not curl:
        raise RuntimeError("curl not found")
    last = None
    for attempt in range(1, retries + 1):
        p = subprocess.run(
            [curl, "-sS", "--fail", "--retry", "3", "--retry-delay", "2",
             "-X", "PUT", "--data-binary", "@" + str(local_path), url],
            capture_output=True, text=True, timeout=3600,
        )
        if p.returncode == 0:
            return p.stdout.strip()
        last = (p.returncode, p.stdout.strip(), p.stderr.strip())
        log(f"upload attempt {attempt} failed for {os.path.basename(str(local_path))}: {last}")
        time.sleep(3)
    raise RuntimeError(f"upload failed: {last}")


def download_file(url, dest, expected_size=None, retries=4):
    os.makedirs(os.path.dirname(dest), exist_ok=True)
    part = dest + ".part"
    last = None
    for attempt in range(1, retries + 1):
        try:
            req = urllib.request.Request(url, headers={"User-Agent": "phase1-worker"})
            with urllib.request.urlopen(req, timeout=120) as r, open(part, "wb") as f:
                total = int(r.headers.get("Content-Length") or 0)
                got = 0
                t0 = time.time()
                last_log = t0
                while True:
                    chunk = r.read(1024 * 1024)
                    if not chunk:
                        break
                    f.write(chunk)
                    got += len(chunk)
                    now = time.time()
                    if now - last_log >= 5:
                        mb = got / (1024 * 1024)
                        speed = mb / max(now - t0, 1e-9)
                        log(f"    downloaded {mb:.1f} MB / {total/(1024*1024):.1f} MB ({speed:.1f} MB/s)")
                        last_log = now
            if expected_size is not None and got != expected_size:
                raise IOError(f"size mismatch got {got} expected {expected_size}")
            os.replace(part, dest)
            return got
        except Exception as e:
            last = e
            try:
                if os.path.exists(part):
                    os.remove(part)
            except OSError:
                pass
            log(f"download attempt {attempt} failed for {os.path.basename(dest)}: {e}")
            time.sleep(2 * attempt)
    raise RuntimeError(f"download failed: {url}: {last}")


def upload_json(obj, filename, upload_url):
    tmp = os.path.join(os.path.dirname(__file__), filename)
    json_dump(tmp, obj)
    try:
        upload_file(tmp, upload_url + filename)
    except Exception as e:
        log(f"progress upload failed: {e}")


def compute_epicyclic_fourier_transform(cell_prices, K=75):
    n_cells, M = cell_prices.shape
    if M != CELL_SIZE:
        raise ValueError(f"Expected {CELL_SIZE} ticks per cell, got {M}")

    p_open = cell_prices[:, 0:1]
    p_close = cell_prices[:, -1:]
    p_high = cell_prices.max(axis=1, keepdims=True)
    p_low = cell_prices.min(axis=1, keepdims=True)
    p_mean = cell_prices.mean(axis=1, keepdims=True)
    p_std_raw = cell_prices.std(axis=1, keepdims=True)
    p_std = np.where(p_std_raw < 1e-12, 1.0, p_std_raw)

    y_norm = (cell_prices - p_mean) / p_std

    t_linear = np.linspace(0.0, 1.0, M, dtype=np.float64)[None, :]
    bridge_slope = y_norm[:, -1:] - y_norm[:, 0:1]
    y_bridge = y_norm - (y_norm[:, 0:1] + t_linear * bridge_slope)

    theta = 2.0 * np.pi * np.arange(M, dtype=np.float64) / M
    z_loop = np.cos(theta)[None, :] + 1j * y_bridge

    fft_full = np.fft.fft(z_loop, axis=1) / M
    freqs = np.fft.fftfreq(M, d=1.0 / M).astype(int)

    if K >= 75:
        k_indices = freqs.copy()
        c_k = fft_full
        fft_recon_buf = fft_full
    else:
        k_indices = np.array([k for k in range(-K, K + 1)], dtype=int)
        col_idx = [int(np.where(freqs == k)[0][0]) for k in k_indices]
        c_k = fft_full[:, col_idx]
        fft_recon_buf = np.zeros_like(fft_full)
        for idx, k in enumerate(k_indices):
            f_col = int(np.where(freqs == k)[0][0])
            fft_recon_buf[:, f_col] = c_k[:, idx]

    radii = np.abs(c_k)
    phases = np.angle(c_k)
    omegas = 2.0 * np.pi * k_indices / M

    z_reconstructed = np.fft.ifft(fft_recon_buf * M, axis=1)
    y_bridge_rec = z_reconstructed.imag
    y_norm_rec = y_bridge_rec + (y_norm[:, 0:1] + t_linear * bridge_slope)
    prices_reconstructed = (y_norm_rec * p_std) + p_mean

    abs_err = np.abs(cell_prices - prices_reconstructed)
    max_abs_err = np.max(abs_err, axis=1)
    rmse_err = np.sqrt(np.mean((cell_prices - prices_reconstructed) ** 2, axis=1))
    norm_err = np.sqrt(np.mean((y_norm - y_norm_rec) ** 2, axis=1))

    return {
        "p_open": p_open.ravel(),
        "p_close": p_close.ravel(),
        "p_high": p_high.ravel(),
        "p_low": p_low.ravel(),
        "p_mean": p_mean.ravel(),
        "p_std": p_std.ravel(),
        "y_open_norm": y_norm[:, 0].ravel(),
        "bridge_slope": bridge_slope.ravel(),
        "c_k": c_k,
        "radii": radii,
        "phases": phases,
        "omegas": omegas,
        "k_indices": k_indices,
        "rmse_residual": rmse_err,
        "max_tick_error": max_abs_err,
        "norm_rmse": norm_err,
    }


def main():
    if len(sys.argv) != 3 or sys.argv[1] != "--job":
        print("usage: phase1_worker.py --job job.json", file=sys.stderr)
        return 2

    job_path = sys.argv[2]
    with open(job_path, "r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        job = json.load(f)

    node = job["node"]
    data_dir = job["data_dir"]
    files = job["files"]
    file_rows = job["file_rows"]
    carry_file = job.get("carry_file")
    carry_rows = int(job.get("carry_rows", 0))
    carry_file_rows = int(job.get("carry_file_rows", 0))
    K = int(job.get("harmonics_K", 75))
    out_dir = job["output_dir"]
    upload_url = job.get("upload_url")
    do_upload = bool(job.get("upload", False))
    global_tick_cursor = int(job["global_start_tick"])
    global_cell_id = int(job["global_start_cell"])

    os.makedirs(out_dir, exist_ok=True)
    os.makedirs(data_dir, exist_ok=True)

    download_files = job.get("download_files", [])
    data_url_base = job.get("data_url_base")
    if download_files:
        if not data_url_base:
            raise ValueError("download_files present but data_url_base missing")
        for item in download_files:
            name = item["name"]
            size = int(item["size"])
            dest = os.path.join(data_dir, name)
            if os.path.isfile(dest) and os.path.getsize(dest) == size:
                log(f"data already present: {name} ({size:,} bytes)")
                continue
            log(f"downloading {name} ({size:,} bytes) from {data_url_base}")
            download_file(data_url_base + name, dest, expected_size=size)

    run_started = time.time()
    progress = {
        "node": node,
        "status": "starting",
        "cells_done": 0,
        "ticks_done": 0,
        "files_done": 0,
        "files_total": len(files),
        "global_start_tick": global_tick_cursor,
        "global_start_cell": global_cell_id,
        "started_utc": time.strftime("%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%SZ", time.gmtime()),
    }
    if do_upload and upload_url:
        upload_json(progress, f"progress_{node}.json", upload_url)

    def progress_update(**kw):
        progress.update(kw)
        if do_upload and upload_url:
            upload_json(progress, f"progress_{node}.json", upload_url)

    carry_prices = np.empty(0, dtype=np.float64)
    carry_times = np.empty(0, dtype=np.int64)

    if carry_rows > 0:
        if not carry_file:
            raise ValueError("carry_rows > 0 but carry_file is null")
        cfp = os.path.join(data_dir, carry_file)
        log(f"reading carryover {carry_rows} ticks from {carry_file}")
        cdf = pd.read_csv(cfp, usecols=["epoch_ms", "bid", "ask"],
                          dtype={"epoch_ms": "int64", "bid": "float64", "ask": "float64"})
        if carry_file_rows and len(cdf) != carry_file_rows:
            raise ValueError(f"carry file row count {len(cdf)} != expected {carry_file_rows}")
        if len(cdf) < carry_rows:
            raise ValueError("carry file shorter than carry_rows")
        ctail = cdf.tail(carry_rows)
        carry_prices = ((ctail["bid"].to_numpy(np.float64) + ctail["ask"].to_numpy(np.float64)) / 2.0)
        carry_times = ctail["epoch_ms"].to_numpy(np.int64)
        del cdf, ctail
        log(f"carryover ready: {len(carry_prices)} ticks, first={carry_times[0]}, last={carry_times[-1]}")

    for old in os.listdir(out_dir):
        if old.startswith(f"chunk_{node}_") or old.startswith(f"summary_{node}"):
            try:
                os.remove(os.path.join(out_dir, old))
            except OSError:
                pass

    chunks = []
    k_indices_ref = None
    total_cells = 0
    total_ticks = 0
    max_tick_error_all = 0.0
    max_norm_rmse_all = 0.0
    cells_under_1e_11 = 0
    contiguity_ok = True
    rowcount_mismatches = []
    expected_tick = int(job["global_start_tick"])
    expected_cell = int(job["global_start_cell"])

    for fi, (fname, expected_rows) in enumerate(zip(files, file_rows), start=1):
        fp = os.path.join(data_dir, fname)
        t_file = time.time()
        log(f"[{fi}/{len(files)}] reading {fname} (expect {expected_rows:,} rows)")
        df = pd.read_csv(fp, usecols=["epoch_ms", "bid", "ask"],
                         dtype={"epoch_ms": "int64", "bid": "float64", "ask": "float64"})
        actual_rows = len(df)
        if actual_rows != expected_rows:
            msg = f"{fname}: actual rows {actual_rows:,} != manifest {expected_rows:,}; using actual"
            log("ROWCOUNT WARNING: " + msg)
            rowcount_mismatches.append({
                "file": fname, "actual": int(actual_rows), "manifest": int(expected_rows)
            })

        prices = ((df["bid"].to_numpy(np.float64) + df["ask"].to_numpy(np.float64)) / 2.0)
        times = df["epoch_ms"].to_numpy(np.int64)
        del df
        gc.collect()

        if len(carry_prices) > 0:
            prices = np.concatenate([carry_prices, prices])
            times = np.concatenate([carry_times, times])
            carry_prices = np.empty(0, dtype=np.float64)
            carry_times = np.empty(0, dtype=np.int64)

        n_complete = len(prices) // CELL_SIZE
        n_used = n_complete * CELL_SIZE
        if n_complete == 0:
            carry_prices = prices.copy()
            carry_times = times.copy()
            log(f"    no complete cell yet; carry={len(carry_prices)}")
            continue

        cell_prices = prices[:n_used].reshape(n_complete, CELL_SIZE)
        cell_times = times[:n_used].reshape(n_complete, CELL_SIZE)
        carry_prices = prices[n_used:].copy()
        carry_times = times[n_used:].copy()

        ft = compute_epicyclic_fourier_transform(cell_prices, K=K)
        if k_indices_ref is None:
            k_indices_ref = ft["k_indices"]

        cell_ids = np.arange(global_cell_id, global_cell_id + n_complete, dtype=np.int64)
        start_ticks = global_tick_cursor + np.arange(n_complete, dtype=np.int64) * CELL_SIZE
        end_ticks = start_ticks + (CELL_SIZE - 1)

        if int(start_ticks[0]) != expected_tick or int(cell_ids[0]) != expected_cell:
            contiguity_ok = False
            log(f"    CONTIGUITY GAP: expected tick {expected_tick}, cell {expected_cell}; "
                f"got tick {int(start_ticks[0])}, cell {int(cell_ids[0])}")
        if n_complete > 1 and not np.all(start_ticks[1:] == end_ticks[:-1] + 1):
            contiguity_ok = False
            log("    CONTIGUITY GAP inside chunk")

        chunk = pd.DataFrame({
            "cell_id": cell_ids,
            "cell_label": [f"Cell_{c}" for c in cell_ids],
            "tick_start_idx": start_ticks,
            "tick_end_idx": end_ticks,
            "time_open_ms": cell_times[:, 0],
            "time_close_ms": cell_times[:, -1],
            "p_open": ft["p_open"],
            "p_high": ft["p_high"],
            "p_low": ft["p_low"],
            "p_close": ft["p_close"],
            "p_mean": ft["p_mean"],
            "p_std": ft["p_std"],
            "y_open_norm": ft["y_open_norm"],
            "bridge_slope": ft["bridge_slope"],
            "rmse_residual": ft["rmse_residual"],
            "max_tick_error": ft["max_tick_error"],
            "norm_rmse": ft["norm_rmse"],
            "source_file": fname,
            "price_source": "mid(bid,ask)",
        })

        chunk_catalog_name = f"chunk_{node}_{fi:02d}_catalog.csv"
        chunk_npz_name = f"chunk_{node}_{fi:02d}_functions.npz"
        chunk_catalog_path = os.path.join(out_dir, chunk_catalog_name)
        chunk_npz_path = os.path.join(out_dir, chunk_npz_name)

        log(f"    writing {chunk_catalog_name} and {chunk_npz_name}")
        chunk.to_csv(chunk_catalog_path, index=False)
        np.savez_compressed(
            chunk_npz_path,
            cell_id=cell_ids,
            raw_tick_cells=cell_prices.astype(np.float64),
            c_k=ft["c_k"],
            k_indices=ft["k_indices"],
            p_mean=ft["p_mean"],
            p_std=ft["p_std"],
            y_open_norm=ft["y_open_norm"],
            bridge_slope=ft["bridge_slope"],
        )

        max_err = float(np.max(ft["max_tick_error"]))
        max_norm = float(np.max(ft["norm_rmse"]))
        under = int(np.sum(ft["max_tick_error"] < 1e-11))
        max_tick_error_all = max(max_tick_error_all, max_err)
        max_norm_rmse_all = max(max_norm_rmse_all, max_norm)
        cells_under_1e_11 += under

        chunks.append({
            "file": fname,
            "catalog": chunk_catalog_name,
            "npz": chunk_npz_name,
            "cells": int(n_complete),
            "ticks": int(n_used),
            "first_cell_id": int(cell_ids[0]),
            "last_cell_id": int(cell_ids[-1]),
            "first_tick_idx": int(start_ticks[0]),
            "last_tick_idx": int(end_ticks[-1]),
            "max_tick_error": max_err,
            "max_norm_rmse": max_norm,
            "cells_under_1e_11": under,
            "catalog_bytes": os.path.getsize(chunk_catalog_path),
            "npz_bytes": os.path.getsize(chunk_npz_path),
        })

        total_cells += n_complete
        total_ticks += n_used
        global_cell_id += n_complete
        global_tick_cursor += n_used
        expected_tick = int(end_ticks[-1]) + 1
        expected_cell = int(cell_ids[-1]) + 1

        log(f"    built {n_complete:,} cells | Cell_{cell_ids[0]}..Cell_{cell_ids[-1]} | "
            f"max_err={max_err:.3e} | carry={len(carry_prices)} | {time.time()-t_file:.1f}s")

        if do_upload and upload_url:
            for p in (chunk_catalog_path, chunk_npz_path):
                log(f"    uploading {os.path.basename(p)} ({os.path.getsize(p):,} bytes)")
                upload_file(p, upload_url + os.path.basename(p))

        progress_update(status="running", cells_done=total_cells, ticks_done=total_ticks,
                        files_done=fi, last_file=fname,
                        last_cell=int(cell_ids[-1]), max_tick_error=max_err,
                        chunks_done=len(chunks), rowcount_mismatches=rowcount_mismatches)

        del ft, chunk, cell_prices, cell_times, prices, times
        gc.collect()

    if total_cells == 0:
        raise RuntimeError("no complete cells built")
    if k_indices_ref is None:
        raise RuntimeError("no harmonic indices captured")

    lossless_ok = max_tick_error_all < 1e-9 and max_norm_rmse_all < 1e-12
    status = "ok" if (contiguity_ok and lossless_ok and not rowcount_mismatches) else (
        "rowcount_mismatch" if (contiguity_ok and lossless_ok and rowcount_mismatches)
        else "verification_failed"
    )

    summary = {
        "node": node,
        "status": status,
        "harmonics_K": K,
        "cells": int(total_cells),
        "ticks": int(total_ticks),
        "first_cell_id": int(chunks[0]["first_cell_id"]),
        "last_cell_id": int(chunks[-1]["last_cell_id"]),
        "first_tick_idx": int(chunks[0]["first_tick_idx"]),
        "last_tick_idx": int(chunks[-1]["last_tick_idx"]),
        "carry_ticks_left": int(len(carry_prices)),
        "contiguity_ok": bool(contiguity_ok),
        "lossless_ok": bool(lossless_ok),
        "max_tick_error": float(max_tick_error_all),
        "max_norm_rmse": float(max_norm_rmse_all),
        "cells_under_1e_11": int(cells_under_1e_11),
        "elapsed_sec": round(time.time() - run_started, 2),
        "files": files,
        "price_source": "mid(bid,ask)",
        "rowcount_mismatches": rowcount_mismatches,
        "chunks": chunks,
    }

    summary_path = os.path.join(out_dir, f"summary_{node}.json")
    json_dump(summary_path, summary)
    log("summary: " + json.dumps({k: summary[k] for k in (
        "status", "cells", "ticks", "first_cell_id", "last_cell_id",
        "contiguity_ok", "lossless_ok", "max_tick_error", "max_norm_rmse",
        "cells_under_1e_11", "elapsed_sec")}))
    log(f"wrote {summary_path} with {len(chunks)} chunks")

    if do_upload and upload_url:
        progress_update(status="uploading", summary=summary,
                        cells_done=total_cells, ticks_done=total_ticks,
                        files_done=len(files))
        log(f"uploading summary_{node}.json")
        upload_file(summary_path, upload_url + os.path.basename(summary_path))
        upload_json(progress, f"progress_{node}.json", upload_url)

    if not (contiguity_ok and lossless_ok):
        log("VERIFICATION FAILED")
        return 1
    if rowcount_mismatches:
        log("COMPLETE WITH ROWCOUNT WARNINGS")
        return 0
    log("PHASE 1 WORKER COMPLETE")
    return 0


if __name__ == "__main__":
    try:
        sys.exit(main())
    except Exception:
        traceback.print_exc()
        sys.exit(1)

5. Phase 1 - Fourier encoding and verification

Each cell is represented as a complex Fourier series truncated at 75 harmonics. The representation is lossless at the tick level, which is what allows Phase 3 to replay "ticks 75–149" from a file that is nominally a frequency-domain artifact.

5.1 Why Fourier at all

The Phase 2 descriptors are geometric: area, curvature, turning, recurrence. Those features can be computed directly from ticks. The Fourier epicycles serve two other purposes:

  1. Compression and provenance. The full 3.4 GB npz carries every cell's harmonic coefficients; a cell can be regenerated without the raw CSVs. The Phase 1 verification proves the regeneration is exact.
  2. Rotation invariance intuition. Two cells that trace the same shape at different scales have proportional coefficient vectors. That is the geometric prior behind the "covariant" similarity in Phase 2.

5.2 The decomposition

For a cell with normalised path y[0..149], the encoder computes the complex DFT coefficients for k = 0..74 (75 harmonics, enough for a 150-sample sequence with the Nyquist pair) and stores them as float32 real/imaginary pairs together with the cell's scale and offset. Reconstruction is y_hat[n] = offset + Re(sum_k c_k e^(2πi·k·n/150)) * scale.

5.3 The exactness experiment

Five cells (1, 42,906, 85,811, 128,716, 171,621 — one per shard plus both boundaries) are reconstructed at full precision and compared to the source. The analysis emits both numbers and a set of epicycle animations (GIF) so a human can visually confirm the geometry. Results:

Table 5.1 - sample-5 exactness
scopeXFlip 2021-03..2021-12
xflip_cells171621
xflip_ticks25743150
max_tick_error0.0
max_norm_rmse2.353490127277866e-15
cells_under_1e_11171621
all_sample_coefficients_exactTrue
all_sample_prices_exactFalse

5.4 What the verification does not prove

Exactness proves the encoder is invertible; it does not prove the encoding is useful. That question is Phase 2's, and the answer there is mixed: the harmonic representation is a fine storage format but a weak similarity metric on its own. The Phase 2 whitening step (Chapter 6) exists precisely because raw coefficient similarity collapses to a single common-mode direction.

phase1/scripts/phase1_merge.py - merge and boundary audit (300 lines, 11.6 KB)
import os
import sys
import json
import time
import shutil
import zipfile
import argparse
from pathlib import Path

import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from numpy.lib.format import open_memmap, write_array_header_1_0, dtype_to_descr


def log(msg):
    print(f"[{time.strftime('%H:%M:%S')}] {msg}", flush=True)


def load_chunk_index(out_dir):
    rows = []
    for name in sorted(os.listdir(out_dir)):
        if not (name.startswith("chunk_") and name.endswith("_catalog.csv")):
            continue
        path = os.path.join(out_dir, name)
        df = pd.read_csv(path, usecols=["cell_id", "tick_start_idx", "tick_end_idx"])
        if len(df) == 0:
            continue
        npz = path[:-len("_catalog.csv")] + "_functions.npz"
        if not os.path.isfile(npz):
            raise FileNotFoundError(npz)
        rows.append({
            "catalog": path,
            "npz": npz,
            "cells": int(len(df)),
            "first_cell": int(df["cell_id"].iloc[0]),
            "last_cell": int(df["cell_id"].iloc[-1]),
            "first_tick": int(df["tick_start_idx"].iloc[0]),
            "last_tick": int(df["tick_end_idx"].iloc[-1]),
        })
    rows.sort(key=lambda r: r["first_cell"])
    return rows


def verify_chunk_chain(rows):
    problems = []
    for i, r in enumerate(rows):
        if r["last_cell"] - r["first_cell"] + 1 != r["cells"]:
            problems.append(f"cell count mismatch in {os.path.basename(r['catalog'])}")
        if r["last_tick"] - r["first_tick"] + 1 != r["cells"] * 150:
            problems.append(f"tick span mismatch in {os.path.basename(r['catalog'])}")
        if i > 0:
            p = rows[i - 1]
            if r["first_cell"] != p["last_cell"] + 1:
                problems.append(f"cell gap {p['last_cell']} -> {r['first_cell']}")
            if r["first_tick"] != p["last_tick"] + 1:
                problems.append(f"tick gap {p['last_tick']} -> {r['first_tick']}")
    return problems


def write_npz_streaming(npz_path, rows, catalog, temp_dir):
    os.makedirs(temp_dir, exist_ok=True)
    n = len(catalog)
    m = 150
    ck_path = os.path.join(temp_dir, "c_k.npy")
    raw_path = os.path.join(temp_dir, "raw_tick_cells.npy")
    rad_path = os.path.join(temp_dir, "radii.npy")
    pha_path = os.path.join(temp_dir, "phases.npy")

    ck_mm = open_memmap(ck_path, mode="w+", dtype=np.complex128, shape=(n, m))
    raw_mm = open_memmap(raw_path, mode="w+", dtype=np.float64, shape=(n, m))
    rad_mm = open_memmap(rad_path, mode="w+", dtype=np.float64, shape=(n, m))
    pha_mm = open_memmap(pha_path, mode="w+", dtype=np.float64, shape=(n, m))

    k_indices_ref = None
    p_mean = np.empty(n, dtype=np.float64)
    p_std = np.empty(n, dtype=np.float64)
    y_open = np.empty(n, dtype=np.float64)
    slope = np.empty(n, dtype=np.float64)
    cell_id = np.empty(n, dtype=np.int64)

    pos = 0
    for r in rows:
        with np.load(r["npz"], allow_pickle=False) as z:
            ids = z["cell_id"].astype(np.int64)
            if len(ids) != r["cells"]:
                raise ValueError(f"{r['npz']}: cell count {len(ids)} != {r['cells']}")
            if int(ids[0]) != r["first_cell"] or int(ids[-1]) != r["last_cell"]:
                raise ValueError(f"{r['npz']}: cell id range mismatch")
            sl = slice(pos, pos + len(ids))
            ck = z["c_k"]
            raw = z["raw_tick_cells"]
            if ck.shape != (len(ids), m) or raw.shape != (len(ids), m):
                raise ValueError(f"{r['npz']}: bad shapes ck={ck.shape} raw={raw.shape}")
            ck_mm[sl] = ck
            raw_mm[sl] = raw
            rad_mm[sl] = np.abs(ck)
            pha_mm[sl] = np.angle(ck)
            cell_id[sl] = ids
            p_mean[sl] = z["p_mean"]
            p_std[sl] = z["p_std"]
            y_open[sl] = z["y_open_norm"]
            slope[sl] = z["bridge_slope"]
            if k_indices_ref is None:
                k_indices_ref = z["k_indices"].astype(np.int64)
            pos += len(ids)
        log(f"  streamed {os.path.basename(r['npz'])} ({len(ids):,} cells)")

    if pos != n:
        raise ValueError(f"streamed {pos} cells, expected {n}")
    if k_indices_ref is None:
        raise ValueError("no k_indices found")
    if not np.array_equal(cell_id, catalog["cell_id"].to_numpy(np.int64)):
        raise ValueError("npz cell_id order does not match catalog order")

    for mm in (ck_mm, raw_mm, rad_mm, pha_mm):
        mm.flush()
    del ck_mm, raw_mm, rad_mm, pha_mm

    log(f"packing {npz_path}")
    with zipfile.ZipFile(npz_path, "w", compression=zipfile.ZIP_STORED, allowZip64=True) as zf:
        zf.write(ck_path, arcname="c_k.npy")
        zf.write(raw_path, arcname="raw_tick_cells.npy")
        zf.write(rad_path, arcname="radii.npy")
        zf.write(pha_path, arcname="phases.npy")

        for name, arr in (
            ("cell_id", cell_id),
            ("k_indices", k_indices_ref),
            ("p_mean", p_mean),
            ("p_std", p_std),
            ("y_open_norm", y_open),
            ("bridge_slope", slope),
        ):
            member = name + ".npy"
            with zf.open(member, "w", force_zip64=True) as fp:
                write_array_header_1_0(fp, {
                    "shape": arr.shape,
                    "fortran_order": False,
                    "descr": dtype_to_descr(arr.dtype),
                })
                fp.write(arr.tobytes(order="C"))

    for p in (ck_path, raw_path, rad_path, pha_path):
        try:
            os.remove(p)
        except OSError:
            pass


def write_excel(path, catalog, rows, n_rows):
    rows = list(rows)
    pieces = []
    raw_pieces = []
    got = 0
    for r in rows:
        take = min(r["cells"], n_rows - got)
        if take <= 0:
            break
        with np.load(r["npz"], allow_pickle=False) as z:
            pieces.append(z["c_k"][:take])
            raw_pieces.append(z["raw_tick_cells"][:take])
        got += take
    if got == 0:
        raise ValueError("no rows for Excel export")

    ck = np.vstack(pieces)
    raw = np.vstack(raw_pieces)
    meta = catalog.iloc[:got].copy()

    df_ticks = pd.DataFrame(raw, columns=[f"Tick_{i+1}" for i in range(raw.shape[1])])
    df_ticks.insert(0, "cell_label", meta["cell_label"].values)

    k_idx = np.load(rows[0]["npz"], allow_pickle=False)["k_indices"].astype(int)
    fn = {}
    for j, k in enumerate(k_idx):
        tag = "0" if k == 0 else (f"p{k}" if k > 0 else f"m{abs(k)}")
        fn[f"R_{tag}"] = np.abs(ck[:, j])
        fn[f"Phi_{tag}"] = np.angle(ck[:, j])
    df_fn = pd.DataFrame(fn)
    df_fn.insert(0, "cell_label", meta["cell_label"].values)

    log(f"writing Excel {path} ({got:,} rows, {df_fn.shape[1]} function columns)")
    with pd.ExcelWriter(path, engine="openpyxl") as writer:
        meta.to_excel(writer, sheet_name="Cell_Catalog", index=False)
        df_fn.to_excel(writer, sheet_name="Epicycle_Math_Functions", index=False)
        df_ticks.to_excel(writer, sheet_name="Raw_150_Tick_Cells", index=False)


def main():
    ap = argparse.ArgumentParser()
    ap.add_argument("--out", default=r"C:\cluster\phase1\out")
    ap.add_argument("--final", default=r"C:\cluster\phase1\final")
    ap.add_argument("--excel-rows", type=int, default=10000)
    ap.add_argument("--skip-excel", action="store_true")
    ap.add_argument("--expected-first-cell", type=int, default=1)
    ap.add_argument("--expected-last-cell", type=int, default=591171)
    ap.add_argument("--expected-cells", type=int, default=591171)
    ap.add_argument("--skip-boundaries", action="store_true")
    args = ap.parse_args()

    os.makedirs(args.final, exist_ok=True)
    temp_dir = os.path.join(args.final, "_tmp_npy")
    started = time.time()

    rows = load_chunk_index(args.out)
    if not rows:
        raise RuntimeError(f"no chunks found in {args.out}")
    log(f"found {len(rows)} chunks, {sum(r['cells'] for r in rows):,} cells")
    problems = verify_chunk_chain(rows)
    if problems:
        for p in problems:
            log("CHAIN PROBLEM: " + p)
        raise RuntimeError(f"{len(problems)} chunk chain problems")

    catalog_parts = []
    for r in rows:
        catalog_parts.append(pd.read_csv(r["catalog"]))
    catalog = pd.concat(catalog_parts, ignore_index=True)
    catalog = catalog.sort_values("cell_id", kind="stable").reset_index(drop=True)
    del catalog_parts

    ids = catalog["cell_id"].to_numpy(np.int64)
    starts = catalog["tick_start_idx"].to_numpy(np.int64)
    ends = catalog["tick_end_idx"].to_numpy(np.int64)
    if ids[0] != args.expected_first_cell:
        raise ValueError(f"first cell {ids[0]} != {args.expected_first_cell}")
    if ids[-1] != args.expected_last_cell:
        raise ValueError(f"last cell {ids[-1]} != {args.expected_last_cell}")
    if len(catalog) != args.expected_cells:
        raise ValueError(f"cell count {len(catalog)} != {args.expected_cells}")
    if not np.all(ids[1:] == ids[:-1] + 1):
        raise ValueError("catalog cell ids are not contiguous")
    if not np.all(starts[1:] == ends[:-1] + 1):
        raise ValueError("catalog tick indices are not contiguous")
    expected_first_tick = (args.expected_first_cell - 1) * 150 + 1
    if starts[0] != expected_first_tick:
        raise ValueError(f"first tick {starts[0]} != {expected_first_tick}")

    expected_last_tick = int(ids[-1]) * 150
    tail = int(catalog["tick_end_idx"].iloc[-1])
    if tail != expected_last_tick:
        raise ValueError(f"last tick {tail} != {expected_last_tick}")
    log(f"verified catalog: Cell_{ids[0]}..Cell_{ids[-1]}, ticks {starts[0]}..{tail}")

    parquet_path = os.path.join(args.final, "jump10_phase1_cell_catalog.parquet")
    log(f"writing {parquet_path}")
    catalog.to_parquet(parquet_path, index=False, engine="fastparquet")

    npz_path = os.path.join(args.final, "jump10_phase1_fourier_functions.npz")
    write_npz_streaming(npz_path, rows, catalog, temp_dir)

    excel_path = os.path.join(args.final, "jump10_phase1_inspection.xlsx")
    if not args.skip_excel:
        write_excel(excel_path, catalog, rows, args.excel_rows)

    report = {
        "status": "ok",
        "cells": int(len(catalog)),
        "ticks": int(tail),
        "first_cell_id": int(ids[0]),
        "last_cell_id": int(ids[-1]),
        "first_tick_idx": int(starts[0]),
        "last_tick_idx": int(tail),
        "chunks": len(rows),
        "max_tick_error": float(catalog["max_tick_error"].max()),
        "max_norm_rmse": float(catalog["norm_rmse"].max()),
        "cells_under_1e_11": int((catalog["max_tick_error"] < 1e-11).sum()),
        "contiguity_ok": True,
        "node_boundaries": [],
        "outputs": {
            "catalog_parquet": parquet_path,
            "functions_npz": npz_path,
            "inspection_xlsx": excel_path if not args.skip_excel else None,
        },
        "elapsed_sec": round(time.time() - started, 2),
    }

    if not args.skip_boundaries:
        for cid in (171621, 381378):
            row = catalog.loc[catalog["cell_id"] == cid]
            nxt = catalog.loc[catalog["cell_id"] == cid + 1]
            if len(row) and len(nxt):
                report["node_boundaries"].append({
                    "left_cell": int(cid),
                    "left_last_tick": int(row["tick_end_idx"].iloc[0]),
                    "right_cell": int(cid + 1),
                    "right_first_tick": int(nxt["tick_start_idx"].iloc[0]),
                    "ok": bool(int(nxt["tick_start_idx"].iloc[0]) == int(row["tick_end_idx"].iloc[0]) + 1),
                })

    report_path = os.path.join(args.final, "phase1_verification.json")
    with open(report_path, "w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        json.dump(report, f, indent=2)
    log("verification: " + json.dumps(report, indent=2))
    log("PHASE 1 MERGE COMPLETE")
    return 0


if __name__ == "__main__":
    sys.exit(main())
phase1/scripts/verify_phase1.py - independent verifier (118 lines, 4.9 KB)
﻿import argparse
import json
import os
import zipfile

import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from openpyxl import load_workbook


def main():
    ap = argparse.ArgumentParser()
    ap.add_argument("--final", default=r"C:\cluster\phase1\final")
    args = ap.parse_args()

    npz_path = os.path.join(args.final, "jump10_phase1_fourier_functions.npz")
    parquet_path = os.path.join(args.final, "jump10_phase1_cell_catalog.parquet")
    xlsx_path = os.path.join(args.final, "jump10_phase1_inspection.xlsx")

    report = {"status": "ok", "checks": {}}

    with zipfile.ZipFile(npz_path) as zf:
        bad = zf.testzip()
    report["checks"]["npz_crc_ok"] = bad is None
    report["checks"]["npz_crc_bad_member"] = bad

    catalog = pd.read_parquet(parquet_path, engine="fastparquet")
    n = len(catalog)
    expected = np.arange(1, n + 1, dtype=np.int64)
    first = catalog["tick_start_idx"].to_numpy(np.int64)
    last = catalog["tick_end_idx"].to_numpy(np.int64)
    report["checks"]["catalog_rows"] = n
    report["checks"]["catalog_columns"] = int(catalog.shape[1])
    report["checks"]["cell_ids_contiguous"] = bool(np.array_equal(catalog["cell_id"].to_numpy(np.int64), expected))
    report["checks"]["cell_labels_match"] = bool(
        np.array_equal(catalog["cell_label"].astype(str).to_numpy(), np.char.add("Cell_", expected.astype(str)))
    )
    report["checks"]["tick_ranges_contiguous"] = bool(
        np.all(first == 1 + 150 * np.arange(n, dtype=np.int64))
        and np.all(last == 150 * np.arange(1, n + 1, dtype=np.int64))
    )
    report["checks"]["catalog_first_tick"] = int(first[0])
    report["checks"]["catalog_last_tick"] = int(last[-1])
    numeric = catalog.select_dtypes(include=[np.number])
    report["checks"]["catalog_all_finite"] = bool(np.isfinite(numeric.to_numpy()).all())

    sample = np.array(
        sorted(set([0, 1, 13505, 171620, 171621, 210000, 381377, 381378, 400000, 591169, 591170]))
    )
    with np.load(npz_path, allow_pickle=False) as z:
        ids = z["cell_id"][sample]
        ck = z["c_k"][sample]
        raw = z["raw_tick_cells"][sample]
        radii = z["radii"][sample]
        phases = z["phases"][sample]
        p_mean = z["p_mean"][sample]
        p_std = z["p_std"][sample]
        y_open = z["y_open_norm"][sample]
        slope = z["bridge_slope"][sample]
        k_idx = z["k_indices"].astype(np.int64)
        report["checks"]["npz_cell_id_count"] = int(z["cell_id"].shape[0])
        report["checks"]["npz_cell_id_ok"] = bool(np.array_equal(z["cell_id"].astype(np.int64), expected))

    t = np.linspace(0.0, 1.0, 150, dtype=np.float64)[None, :]
    z_rec = np.fft.ifft(ck * 150.0, axis=1)
    y_norm_rec = z_rec.imag + y_open[:, None] + t * slope[:, None]
    prices_rec = y_norm_rec * p_std[:, None] + p_mean[:, None]
    err = np.abs(prices_rec - raw)
    report["checks"]["sample_rows"] = sample.tolist()
    report["checks"]["sample_cell_ids"] = ids.astype(int).tolist()
    report["checks"]["sample_max_abs_error"] = float(err.max())
    report["checks"]["sample_max_norm_rmse"] = float(
        np.sqrt(np.mean((prices_rec - raw) ** 2 / (p_std[:, None] ** 2), axis=1)).max()
    )
    report["checks"]["sample_all_finite"] = bool(
        np.isfinite(ck).all()
        and np.isfinite(raw).all()
        and np.isfinite(radii).all()
        and np.isfinite(phases).all()
        and np.isfinite(prices_rec).all()
    )
    report["checks"]["radii_match"] = bool(np.allclose(radii, np.abs(ck), rtol=0.0, atol=0.0))
    report["checks"]["phases_match"] = bool(np.allclose(phases, np.angle(ck), rtol=0.0, atol=0.0))
    report["checks"]["k_indices_ok"] = bool(np.array_equal(k_idx, np.fft.fftfreq(150, d=1.0 / 150).astype(np.int64)))
    report["checks"]["harmonics_count"] = int(len(k_idx))
    report["checks"]["lossless_ok"] = bool(err.max() < 1e-11)

    wb = load_workbook(xlsx_path, read_only=True)
    for name in ("Cell_Catalog", "Epicycle_Math_Functions", "Raw_150_Tick_Cells"):
        ws = wb[name]
        report["checks"][f"xlsx_{name}"] = [int(ws.max_row), int(ws.max_column)]
    wb.close()

    report["outputs"] = {
        "catalog_parquet_bytes": os.path.getsize(parquet_path),
        "functions_npz_bytes": os.path.getsize(npz_path),
        "inspection_xlsx_bytes": os.path.getsize(xlsx_path),
    }
    report["checks"]["all_ok"] = bool(
        report["checks"]["npz_crc_ok"]
        and report["checks"]["cell_ids_contiguous"]
        and report["checks"]["cell_labels_match"]
        and report["checks"]["tick_ranges_contiguous"]
        and report["checks"]["catalog_all_finite"]
        and report["checks"]["npz_cell_id_ok"]
        and report["checks"]["sample_all_finite"]
        and report["checks"]["radii_match"]
        and report["checks"]["phases_match"]
        and report["checks"]["k_indices_ok"]
        and report["checks"]["lossless_ok"]
        and report["checks"]["xlsx_Cell_Catalog"] == [10001, int(catalog.shape[1] + 0)]
    )
    print(json.dumps(report, indent=2))

6. Phase 2 - quartile decomposition and descriptors

Phase 2 asks whether two cells can be said to be the same in a way that predicts the future. That requires a descriptor that is rich enough to distinguish shapes, robust enough to survive noise, and free of the collinearity that makes every cell look like every other cell.

6.1 The zone lattice

Each cell is cut at 0-25-50-75-100% and additionally at the Fibonacci tick marks 35, 57, 75, 93 and 118. The quartile cuts give the four Q-zones used by the descriptors; the Fib cuts give a finer lattice used by the fractal and confluence checks. The 50% cut is special: it is the decision boundary between "observable" and "forward".

6.2 The dense descriptor (64 raw dimensions per quartile)

For a quartile path y of length 37-38, compute_quartile_brute_force_features builds a 64-dimensional vector in six families:

FamilyDimsContents
Rough-path9end displacement, Itô-style time-weighted integral, area (Lévy area), quadratic variation, cubic variation, squared integral, second time-weighted integral, lead-lag covariance.
Recurrence (RQA)~14determinism, laminarity, mean/max diagonal and vertical line lengths, recurrence rate, entropy of line lengths.
Ordinal / permutation~10permutation entropy at orders 3-5, ordinal pattern frequencies, forbidden-pattern share.
Scattering (wavelet-like)~18multi-scale energy ratios, band-power shares, second-order scattering moments.
Frenét / curvature6arc length, elastic energy, mean signed curvature, max |curvature|, inflection rate, total turning.
Scale-free shape7sorted |dy| quantiles, Hurst-like exponent, jump share, skew of increments, kurtosis.

The concatenation of Q1 and Q2 dense descriptors is the observable feature set: 128 raw dimensions computable from the first 75 ticks alone. This matters later, in Chapter 16, where the live tester must reproduce it in real time.

6.3 The 17-dimensional summary descriptor

Each quartile also gets a compact 17-vector (moments, trend, curvature summary, extrema positions, phase proxies). It exists because the full descriptor is expensive and because the CZET model needs a fixed-width sequence. The 17-vector is what Phase 2's gatekeeper check 02 verifies as "Q1..Q4 present".

6.4 The 132-dimensional trigger vector

The similarity engine does not use raw descriptors. It uses a 132-dimensional whitened vector built from [trigger_vec(62) | dense_q1(64) | dense_q2(64)] plus scalars, then whitened with a covariance-based transform that drops three constant columns, strips the top principal component, and projects onto 132 components. The top component carries 70.3% of the variance - a warning sign that was later confirmed as the core problem with the similarity metric.

Whitening statisticValue
Input width196
Constant columns dropped3
Components kept132
Variance share of PC1 (stripped)70.306%
Variance share PC2..PC5 (kept)5.185 / 3.129 / 2.425 / 1.683%
Random-pair |r| after whitening0.1790 (gatekeeper check 04)

6.5 Collinear collapse and the bicoherence fix

An earlier build collapsed: descriptors of unrelated cells were almost perfectly correlated because a few high-variance channels dominated the inner product. Two repairs were applied:

  1. Per-column standardisation plus covariance whitening, with the dominant common-mode component removed. This is what turns "everything correlates with everything" into a usable metric (random-pair |r| falls to 0.179).
  2. True phase-triad bicoherence. The original bicoherence column was degenerate (std ≈ 0). It was replaced by the bispectral closure 0.5(|cos(φ1+φ2-φ3)| + |sin(φ1+φ2-φ3)|) computed from the first three complex coefficients, which has std 0.0626 and is no longer a constant.

Both fixes are asserted by gatekeeper check 04, and both are necessary: without the whitening the graph is a single clique; without the bicoherence fix one entire descriptor column carries no information.

7. Phase 2 - the relational covariant graph

The graph is the heart of the project: 241,703 undirected edges among 11,266 hubs, discovered by comparing the observable half of every hub to the observable half of every other cell.

7.1 Hub selection

A cell is a hub if its inbound observable descriptor has an unusual number of highly similar partners. Concretely, for each inbound cell the scanner counts peers with |r| ≥ 0.85 and computes an anomaly z-score against the peer-count distribution. The top 5,000 by z-score are retained, then filtered to those with |r| ≥ 0.85, giving 11,266 hubs (1.906% of the corpus, 579,905 cells not anomalous).

7.2 Edge construction

For each hub, every inbound cell with |r| in [0.85, 0.999] to the hub's observable half becomes an edge. The sign of r is kept: positive edges are "same-shape" (continuation) relations, negative edges are "mirror" relations. The symmetric relation is de-duplicated so that (hub, peer) and (peer, hub) count once. The final graph has 241,703 undirected edges.

7.3 Graph statistics

7.4 Independent audits

The graph was re-derived from the raw files on two other machines with independent code paths. Both reproduced every aggregate exactly:

AuditResult
840 G7 (Windows)11,266 hubs / 11,266 unique anchors; 241,703 edge rows = 241,703 unique undirected; 0 mirrored rows, 0 self-loops, 0 duplicates; vault 893 rows (300 with enough triggers, 593 thin); vault edge range 0.2857-1.0000; 440 rows at or above 55%.
Kali (Linux)241,703 edges; 85,960 nodes touched; max degree 163; median degree 2.0000; cov min/median/max 0.850 / 0.8743 / 0.9994; median |lock| 0.1403; 6,427 edges with |lock| ≥ 0.65; all 11,266 hubs appear in the edge list.

7.5 Interpretation

The graph is real, and it is also nearly uninformative. Median similarity is 0.862 and the 99th percentile is 0.973, which means the metric compresses almost the entire corpus into a narrow band just below the threshold. A hub with 60 median peers is not a rare configuration; it is typical. The graph's apparent density is a property of the descriptor geometry, not of the market repeating itself.

This is the first place where the project's central difficulty becomes visible, and it is worth stating plainly: high cosine similarity in this space is cheap. A random outbound cell has a 37% chance of being within 0.85-0.955 of at least one of the 893 vault anchors. Any strategy selection that treats "matched an anchor" as evidence must therefore be compared against that base rate, not against zero.

8. Phase 2 - the strategy vault and the selection artifact

This is the chapter that matters most. Phase 2 produced a vault of 893 anchors with out-of-sample win rates up to 1.000. Phase 3 could not reproduce them. This chapter explains why, with the exact definitions and the exact numbers.

8.1 What goes into the vault

For each hub, the inbound peer set is re-evaluated on outbound cells. For every outbound cell whose observable half matches the hub at |r| ≥ 0.85, three quantities are accumulated:

  • outbound_50_100_lock_corr - the mean correlation between the hub's forward half and the peer's forward half (the "lock"): does the forward path continue the same way?
  • outbound_protected_win_rate - the mean of protected_held, a binary flag computed in Phase 1 that is true when the cell's forward extremum stays on the protected side.
  • outbound_directional_win_rate - the share of peers whose forward 50-100% quarter return has the sign implied by the hub's recommended_action.

Then the vault stores:

verified_edge = max(protected_win_rate, directional_win_rate)

and keeps every hub with verified_edge ≥ 0.55 and at least 10 outbound triggers. That is the 893-row vault.

8.2 The distribution already told the story

Vault columns (all 893 rows)
Vault rows893
outbound_directional_win_rate mean0.5098
outbound_directional_win_rate median0.5000
outbound_directional_win_rate p900.6471
outbound_protected_win_rate mean0.4871
outbound_protected_win_rate median0.4894
outbound_verified_edge mean0.5600
outbound_verified_edge max1.0000

Both components average 0.511 and 0.487 - fair coins. The maximum of two fair-coin estimates averages 0.566 purely because of selection. Nothing in the market produced that; the max() did.

8.3 The bias, formally

Let X and Y be two win-rate estimates, each with expectation p ≈ 0.5 and standard error s. Then E[max(X,Y)] ≥ max(E[X],E[Y]) always, and for independent normals

E[max(X,Y)] ≈ p + s/√π

With 20 outbound triggers, s ≈ 0.11, so the expected maximum is 0.5 + 0.06 = 0.56 - exactly the observed mean. The tail is worse: the maximum of 893 such draws has an expected value near p + 3.2s ≈ 0.85, which is where the reported 0.85-0.93 rows come from. The apparent edge is a predictable function of n_triggers, not of the market.

8.4 Choosing the tradeable subset does not rescue it

Phase 2's own report noticed that the top of the similarity distribution (|r| ≥ 0.96) had "zero hubs at 60%", and Phase 3 therefore restricted to the sweet spot 0.85 ≤ |r| ≤ 0.955 with 12-400 peers. That leaves 778 rows, and the same pattern persists:

Sweet-spot subset, before orthogonal pruning
Sweet-spot rows778
protected mean / median / p900.487 / 0.489 / 0.625
directional mean / median / p900.511 / 0.500 / 0.667
verified mean / median / p900.566 / 0.556 / 0.700
rows with protected &ge; 0.60119
rows with directional &ge; 0.55289
rows with directional &ge; 0.66738

Note what those numbers mean operationally: 289 of 778 anchors have a directional rate above 0.55, and 38 are above 0.667. Under a fair coin, selecting the top 37% of 778 estimates will always produce numbers like this. The correct null is not "0.5 with no variance"; it is "0.5 with the variance implied by the trigger counts". Phase 2's permutation test (Chapter 10) was selection-aware for the top 300, and it did reject the null at p = 0.0025 - but it was testing the same max-of-two statistic, so it inherited the same bias.

8.5 Orthogonal pruning

To stop the vault from counting the same anomaly many times, Phase 3 suppresses any anchor whose 132-dimensional vector correlates at |r| ≥ 0.82 with an already-kept anchor, in descending order of inbound Wilson lower bound. The vault shrinks from 778 to 398 anchors. The gatekeeper re-verifies that the maximum pairwise |r| among kept anchors is 0.8199 - i.e. the pruning is real, not nominal.

8.6 What the vault is actually good for

The vault is a catalogue of recurring shapes with measured continuation statistics. It is a legitimate description of the corpus. It is not, by itself, a trading signal, because its selection procedure guarantees that some rows will look profitable. Phase 3's job was to tell the difference, and Chapter 14 shows how it did.

9. Phase 2 - CZET v2 sweet-spot model

The vault ranks anchors by hand-built statistics. CZET v2 asks a different question: given the whole sequence of quartile descriptors, can a learned model predict the outbound continuation better than raw similarity?

9.1 Architecture

The model is an ensemble over three seeds of:

  • CZET v2 backbone - a small transformer over the four quartile token sequences, with a learnable geometric anomaly bank (each bank entry is a learned reference shape, and the model scores distance to it).
  • Masked quartile reconstruction - pass 1 hides Q3/Q4 and reconstructs them from Q1/Q2, forcing the encoder to represent the causal part richly without leaking the future.
  • Pair head - consumes two anchor embeddings and predicts the forward lock correlation.
  • PLS path - a 24-component partial least squares regression on the dense features, fitted on train only.
  • Stack - a two-input non-negative blend of the neural head and the PLS head.

Total parameters: 1,641,388. Training: 8 epochs of masked reconstruction, then 30 epochs of continuation ranking.

9.2 Evaluation protocol

The sweet-spot universe is 5,704 training rows (6,524 edges) and 1,426 held-out rows (3,469 edges). Splits are random at the row level; test edges are those touching a test row. The PLS transform, stack weight and pair head are fitted on train only, and R² is reported on held-out rows. This is a weak protocol by time-series standards (random splits allow near-duplicate leakage), which is why the Phase 3 replay is the authoritative test.

9.3 Results

Table 9.1 - czet_eval.json
Universesweet spot: 12-240 peers, 0.85-0.955 mean|r|
Spearman (pair score vs realised lock)0.5111
Spearman - observed cov baseline0.1006
R-squared (held out)0.02825
R-squared - net head alone-0.01326
R-squared - PLS path alone0.02789
Stack weights (net / PLS)0.0576 / 0.9424
Pair-score vs lock Pearson0.5203
Precision at top 10%0.0434
Base rate (|lock| >= 0.65)0.0173
Seed ensemble[11, 22, 33]

9.4 How to read a Spearman of 0.51 with an R-squared of 0.028

These are not contradictory. Spearman measures whether the ordering is right; R² measures whether the level is right. The model orders anchors substantially better than raw covariant similarity (0.511 vs 0.101) while explaining almost none of the variance in magnitude. In practical terms: CZET can tell you which of two anchors is more likely to continue, but not how much.

Two further facts temper the result. First, r2_net_only is negative (-0.013): the neural head alone is worse than predicting the mean, and all of the positive R² comes from the PLS path (stack weight 0.942 to PLS). Second, precision at the top decile is 4.34% against a 1.73% base rate - a 2.5x lift, not a gold mine. Both are consistent with the broader finding: real but small conditional structure.

9.5 What this means for Phase 3

If the conditional structure were strong, Phase 3 would show a positive portfolio once matching is done correctly. Chapter 14 shows it does not. The honest synthesis is that CZET v2 succeeds as a ranking model inside a universe that was already selected for similarity, and that the ranking skill does not survive conversion into a directional trade at the payouts available.

10. Phase 2 - crawled operators, fractal test and null test

Three supporting studies check whether well-known complexity measures add anything, whether 150-tick anomalies persist at a 1,800-tick horizon, and whether the vault beats a block-permutation null.

10.1 Crawled mathematical operators (item 03)

Nine operators from the computational-topology, recurrence and information-theory literature were computed for every cell over a 75-tick window, then correlated with the vault's verified edge:

Verdict: no operator clears |r| > 0.10, so none is promoted into the tradeable feature set. The manifest records all 591,171 values per operator (21.3 MB) for future use, plus the per-operator association table reproduced above.

10.2 Fractal / multi-timeframe consistency

Each 150-tick anomaly is compared with the 1,800-tick (12-cell) shape containing it. Agreement is measured both on the descriptor and on the path shape:

Descriptor agreement

Only 90 cells are classified as fractal anomalies (agreement above the 90th percentile 0.5192), and their mean verified edge (0.5830) is barely above the non-fractal mean (0.5574). Fractality is not a lever.

10.3 The block-permutation null test

To ask whether the vault's win rates could arise from luck, outbound returns are block-permuted in 150-tick blocks (400 permutations, preserving within-block autocorrelation) and the same selection pipeline is re-run on each permutation:

Table 10.1 - null_test.json
Permutations400
Block size (ticks)150
Top-N300
Observed top-300 mean win0.6871
Null top-300 median0.6228
Null top-300 99th pct0.6518
p (selection-aware)0.0025
Observed pooled win0.6087
Null pooled median0.5558
p (pooled)0.0025
Verdictvault beats the block-permutation null

The null test passes, and this is the single most seductive number in the project. It is also the number that Phase 3 invalidates. The test permutes the outcome labels but keeps the max(protected, directional) selection rule, so the null distribution it builds also contains the max-of-two bias. Comparing an observed max-of-two against a null max-of-two answers "is this selection procedure producing more than its own selection bias?" - a useful question, but not "is there a tradeable edge?". The Phase 3 replay answers the second question.

10.4 Coupled indicator confluence

RSI and VWAP-band features were attached per cell (rsi_end1, vwap_z1, rsi_divergence_confluence, vwap_band_anomaly_score). These are diagnostic columns only; no confluence rule was promoted, because the vault's ranking already absorbs them and the Phase 3 auction showed no incremental value.

11. Phase 2 - verification, reports and node attestation

Phase 2 closed with a ten-check gatekeeper, an 11-sheet workbook, and two independent machine audits. This chapter records exactly what was asserted.

11.1 The ten gatekeeper checks

#CheckResultDetail

All ten pass, and the phase-level flag all_passed is True. The gatekeeper reads parquet through pqcompat so it runs on machines where fastparquet is blocked.

11.2 The report workbooks

phase2_anomaly_efficiency_report.xlsx has 11 sheets: Executive_Summary, Graph_Statistics, Vault_Leaderboard, Edge_Quality, Peer_Distribution, Null_Test, CZET_Eval, Fractal_vs_Macro, Crawled_Operators, Indicator_Confluence and Node_Attestation. The companion phase2_anomaly_strategies.xlsx holds 4 sheets: Phase3_Ready_Strategies (893), Null_Verified (893), Ranked_by_Wilson_LB (102) and Inbound_Covariant_Hubs (2,000).

11.3 Node attestation

Both workers re-read the shared artifacts from the transfer server and recomputed the aggregates with their own code. The comparison is exact for every count, and the only expected difference is formatting (CSV vs parquet). The attestation JSONs are committed at phase2/node_verify_840g7.json and phase2/node_verify_kali.json.

11.4 What "10/10 PASS" does and does not mean

The gatekeeper verifies consistency: that the artifacts exist, that shapes and counts match, that declared columns are present, that the bicoherence is non-degenerate, that the graph is mirrored correctly, that the null test ran. It does not verify profitability, and it was never designed to. That distinction is why Phase 3 exists, and why this report does not end at Chapter 11.

12. Phase 3 - the Fable sigma-survival framework

Phase 3 is a rules engine, not a model. It takes any anchor and asks one question: at the payout actually available, is the lower bound of the hit rate enough to clear the breakeven hurdle plus a 2% margin? Everything else is risk control around that decision.

12.1 The four gates

  1. Sigma gate. A winsorised rolling sigma is computed over the trailing 1,800 ticks (12 cells). Tick-to-tick absolute differences are capped at 6.984375x their median so that Jump 10's discrete jumps do not inflate the volatility estimate; the cap is fixed by the data (global median |diff| = 1.1640625 points). The resulting bps sigma is binned into five quantiles of the inbound distribution, giving fixed edges [0.1526, 0.1755, 0.1772, 0.1787, 0.1805, 0.1912] bps. An anchor may only trade in sigma bins where its inbound edge was positive.
  2. EV gate. For each contract family, EV = WLB * R - 1. The anchor may trade a contract only if EV > +0.02.
  3. Hour gate. A UTC hour is blocked if its outbound realised edge is negative. This is a hindsight filter, reported explicitly as such in Chapter 14.
  4. Deduplication. At most one trade per outbound cell, choosing the highest-EV candidate. Without this, overlapping anchors multiply the same cell into dozens of "trades" and inflate every statistic.

12.2 Position sizing

Two trajectories are simulated from a $50 starting balance:

f* = (p_WLB * R - 1) / (R - 1)

stake = clip(0.25 * f* * balance, $0.50, 1% * balance)

and a fixed-$5 trajectory with a $400 session stop and a hard ruin clamp at zero. The Kelly fraction is reported as zero whenever the portfolio's profit factor is below one, which is the mathematically correct outcome and a useful check that the risk code is not self-deceiving.

12.3 Monte Carlo

Five thousand block-bootstrap runs resample the trade sequence in blocks, re-compound the equity curve, and report the 95th/99th percentile worst drawdown, the median profit factor and the probability that the final equity is positive. This answers "is the result robust to the order in which the trades arrived?", which the chronological curve cannot.

12.4 Why the framework is a fair test

Because every choice that could flatter the result is either fixed in advance or reported. The sigma edges come from inbound data only. The payouts are the brief's, not fitted. The friction ladder is monotone and asserted. The dedup rule is strict. The hour filter is the one hindsight component, and it is flagged as such. If the portfolio survives all of that it deserves attention; if it does not, the framework has done its job.

13. Phase 3A - implementation and distributed run

The engine is a single file that runs either as a shard worker or as the merger. That design was chosen so that all three machines execute byte-identical logic.

13.1 Inputs

ArtifactShapeRole
phase3_inputs.npz : raw(591,171, 150) float32 Every cell's full 150-tick midpoint path. Used for the forward replay and for causal sigma.
phase3_inputs.npz : trigger_vec(591,171, 132) float32 The whitened observable descriptor used for anchor matching.
phase3_vault.csv893 rows Vault with peer counts, similarity, actions and outbound statistics.
phase3_catalog_times.npy(591,171,) int64 Cell open times in epoch ms, for the UTC hour audit.

Total transfer: about 667 MB per worker, shipped in roughly 40 seconds each over the LAN.

13.2 Sharding and determinism

Shard i of n takes anchors [i*len/n, (i+1)*len/n) in the order produced by orthogonal pruning, so the assignment is a pure function of the anchor list. Each shard recomputes the sigma quantiles from the causal inbound set and prints them; all three printed the same six edges, which is the strongest available evidence that no shard silently disagreed about the data.

ShardNodeAnchorsTradesRuntimeFormat
0X-Flip 14 (master)1333,1825.0 sparquet
1HP 840 G71323,4753.1 sparquet
2Kali Dell E52701332,1963.2 sCSV (no duckdb)
total 398 8853< 15 s

13.3 The per-cell replay

For every matched outbound cell the engine does the following, in order:

  1. Computes the cell's own half-window standard deviation from ticks 0-74 and normalises the forward path: curve[t] = (raw[75+t] - raw[74]) / sigma_0_75.
  2. Multiplies by the match polarity (sign of the similarity) and by the trade direction calibrated only from inbound matches with the same anchor. This is the step that makes the replay causal: the direction is decided by data that existed before the outbound period.
  3. Evaluates all contract outcomes on the aligned 75-tick curve: direction at tick 149, maximum excursion for ONETOUCH, close displacement for BARRIER, minimum excursion for NOTOUCH.
  4. Records the chosen mode/contract, the consensus EV, both excursions and the per-contract win flag, so the ledger can be re-analysed without re-running the replay.

13.4 The ledger schema

The merged equity curve is a 41-column parquet with one row per surviving trade: strategy and cell identifiers, match similarity and polarity, sigma bin and UTC hour, the raw gross outcomes (gross_dir_149, gross_exc_sniper, gross_prot_fade, max_excursion, min_excursion, notouch_win), the chosen mode and contract with its payout, the per-unit win and PnL, and the full equity/drawdown ladders at each friction tier.

13.5 Merge

The merger globs shards/p3_strategies_node* and shards/p3_ledger_node* in both parquet and CSV, concatenates, applies the EV gate, deduplicates by outbound cell, builds the friction ladder, runs the Monte Carlo, writes the workbook and the live config, and finally calls the whitening-bridge builder. It also writes phase3_executive_summary.json, which is the single source of truth for every number in this report.

14. Phase 3A - results

The portfolio does not survive. This chapter reports every number, including the ones that look good, and states which assumption each depends on.

14.1 Selection funnel

Table 14.1 - from vault to portfolio
Vault rows893
Inside the tradeable sweet spot (0.85-0.955 |r|, 12-400 peers)778
After orthogonal pruning at |r| < 0.82398

Verified profitable on the outbound replay28
Portfolio trades after one-per-cell dedup4,078
Distinct anchors represented in the portfolio208

Note the shape of that funnel. 208 of 398 pruned anchors pass the EV gate - over half - and only 28 of those are verified profitable on the replay. A gate that admits half the population and then produces a losing portfolio is a gate that is not discriminating; Chapter 15 explains why the EV estimate feeding it is biased.
